## AI Research

### [Gradient Routing Localizes and Removes Unintended Behaviors in RL](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mKJEBiuuxHKuCoTB6/gradient-routing-localizes-and-removes-unintended-behaviors)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Authors: Shawn Hu*, Jake Ward*, Aria Wong, Nathan Hu, Bryce Woodworth, Alexander Matt Turner, Alex Cloud (*Equal contribution, randomly ordered)</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">tl;dr.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> During training, models sometimes learn to cheat at tasks rather than solve them in the intended way, a problem known as reward hacking. One mitigation is to use an automated monitor to detect and penalize hacks, but this can teach the model to hack in ways the monitor can’t detect. We propose GRAFT, which instead confines the learning updates from detected hacks to a designated set of parameters that we remove after training. We find that removing these parameters can prevent reward hacks, including hacks that the monitors can’t detect. In our experiments, which are limited to small models and simple monitors, this method prevents reward hacks while still teaching the model to perform the intended task.</span></p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17062"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">📄Paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://github.com/jnward/GRAFT"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">💻Code</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, 🐦</span><a href="https://x.com/_jake_ward/status/2112264244767211854" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Twitter</span></a></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Research done as part of </span></i><a href="https://www.matsprogram.org/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MATS</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This article is cross-posted to the </span></i><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/graft/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic Alignment Science Blog</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Background</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reinforcement learning (RL) is an important component of frontier language model training. During RL, a model attempts to complete tasks given by a developer, and attempts are scored by a reward function. Sometimes models can find ways to “cheat” at tasks, performing behaviors that score highly without solving the task in the intended way. This phenomenon is called “reward hacking”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model cards accompanying the release of </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/claude-opus-5-5-system-card"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;</span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-5-6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">frontier models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;show these models reward hack. As model capabilities have increased, so too have the </span><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2025-06-05-recent-reward-hacking/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sophistication and variety of actual reward hacks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, in some cases </span><a href="https://cdn.openai.com/pdf/67869394-cb91-4c12-888c-5cbd85c7814c/OpenAI-Hugging-Face%20Incident-Technical-Report.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">causing real-world incidents</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This limits the usefulness and reliability of AI systems, and poses safety risks: research suggests that reward hacking could generalize to dangerous misbehaviors, including violating restrictions on aiding various harmful activities, </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">autonomously conducting cyberattacks</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, deception, and </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.18397"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">research sabotage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One way to prevent reward hacking is by introducing monitors to the RL training loop. These monitors, which could be LLM judges or keyword detectors, read the content of trajectories generated during training and flag trajectories containing undesired behaviors. When a trajectory is flagged, a reward penalty can be assigned, which prevents those behaviors from being reinforced.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, monitors make errors, and incorporating monitor verdicts into reward functions can reinforce </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.11926"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monitor-exploiting policies</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;that misbehave in ways that go undetected. As a result, monitors that are useful at the beginning of training may become blind to reward hacking later in training. For example, policies trained against LLM judges can learn to reward hack in ways </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2407.13692"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">too subtle for the judge to catch</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, trick judges by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17776"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">impersonating them</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or circumvent them by </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">agentic means</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The method</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We explore a potential solution to reward hacking that we call GRAFT: Gradient Routing Adapter Fine-Tuning. GRAFT is an adaptation of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.04332"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gradient routing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;for post-training. The idea is to constrain weight updates to an adapter when we detect undesired behaviors. In doing so, we localize representations of such behaviors to that adapter. This allows us to remove the behaviors when we deploy the model, simply by ablating the adapter.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concretely, before RL begins, we initialize two sets of additional neurons in each MLP layer, and designate one the RETAIN adapter and one the FORGET adapter. During training, when a rollout trajectory is flagged by the monitor, we zero-mask gradients to the RETAIN adapter, updating only the neurons in the FORGET adapter. After training, we ablate the FORGET neurons to remove the undesired behavior while preserving performance on target tasks (Figure 1). GRAFT can also be used with full-parameter fine-tuning: the FORGET adapter is kept, and the full parameters of the base model are trained in place of the RETAIN adapter.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Importantly, we use the monitor only to decide which parameters are updated, and not to specify the reward. Consequently, policies that exploit weaknesses of the monitor are not differentially reinforced. As a result, GRAFT allows developers to use weak monitors for supervision during training without training the model to exploit the monitor's weaknesses.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/mKJEBiuuxHKuCoTB6/daypl315yci67xbhx7dv" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 1. </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gradient Routing Adapter Fine-Tuning (GRAFT).</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &nbsp;Additional neurons are added to each MLP in a decoder-only Transformer; these are partitioned into RETAIN and FORGET. When training in the target environment (left, center), both adapters are enabled for both the rollout phase and the actor update's forward pass. On trajectories not flagged by the monitor (left), both the RETAIN and FORGET adapters receive gradient updates. On trajectories flagged by the monitor (center), only the FORGET adapter is updated and the gradient update to this adapter is amplified by a constant factor. We concurrently train the model in a separate anchor environment where only the RETAIN adapter is enabled and updated (right).</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are two noteworthy implementation details:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because GRAFT trains with the FORGET adapter but deploys the model without it, there is a mismatch between training and deployment that could hurt performance. To address this, we rely on an “anchor environment” to teach the model to act in a coherent way when the FORGET adapter is ablated. In the experiment we present below, the anchor environment is similar to the target environment in that it teaches a coding task, but the task is otherwise unrelated to the task in the target environment. Training in this environment alone only slightly improves the model’s performance on tasks in the target environment.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On flagged samples, fewer total parameters are updated. As a heuristic way of updating the network an equal amount for such samples, we scale the size of updates on these parameters by a proportionate factor.</span></li></ol><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Results</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this blog post, we present a single result from our paper. We apply GRAFT to Qwen3-8B in the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.07084"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Countdown-Code</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;RLVR environment from Khalifa et al.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Countdown is a simple game in which the model is given a list of integers and a target quantity, and is asked to find an arithmetic expression which evaluates to the target quantity and uses each of the integers exactly once. The prompt describes this game and supplies a scaffolding “solution function” (with the expression left blank) and a “test function” (which checks the constraints and the evaluation of the expression). The model is asked to output the same two functions with the correct expression filled in. The environment assigns full reward if the model returns a solution function that passes its returned test function. We measure the rate at which the model produces a solution function that passes both the model-supplied test function and a held-out test function.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The following types of reward hack are possible in this environment:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Replacing the returned test function with one that trivially passes;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modifying the returned test function to remove the constraint check so that integers can be used more than once; and</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Modifying the inputs in the returned solution function so that a different, easier problem is specified, and then solving that problem.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All prompts in this environment admit all three types of hacks. Our experiments use a monitor that detects all hacks of type 1, and no other hacks.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/mKJEBiuuxHKuCoTB6/e8l7kfr0l0e392nnvdy6" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Figure 2. </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GRAFT localizes and removes reward hacking in Qwen3-8B trained in an arithmetic reasoning environment. Left:</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &nbsp;GRAFT outperforms all tested methods on reward hack rate and achieves competitive performance on the target task. "Clean environment solve rate" represents models' solve rates on a variant of the environment without the test and solution scaffolds, in which reward hacking is structurally impossible. "Reward hack rate" represents the rate of reward hacking the model exhibits on prompts where hacking is possible. </span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Right:</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &nbsp;Performance of the GRAFT-trained model in different configurations as training progresses. We measure performance with both adapters, only the RETAIN adapter (deployment configuration) and only the FORGET adapter (a diagnostic configuration to get an idea of what the FORGET adapter has learned). By the end of training, reward hacking behavior is entirely localized to the FORGET adapter. All error bars and shaded bands are standard errors of the mean over seeds.</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When no intervention is applied, the model learns to apply hack 1 on the majority of trajectories. When a reward penalty is applied on trajectories that the monitor flags, the model learns to apply hack 2 on about 30% of trajectories and never apply hack 1 (Figure 2, left). The result is qualitatively similar regardless of penalty size. Notably, since hacks of other types would not be detected by the monitor,&nbsp;a practitioner might incorrectly conclude that this policy is hack-free.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After training with GRAFT, the pre-ablation model performs type-1 reward hacks on up to 35% of trajectories. Ablating the FORGET adapter drops the rate of hacking (of all types) to nearly zero, while the rate of correct solutions on the target task remains nearly 20pp higher than that of the base model (Figure 2). Notably, hacks of undetectable types do not emerge in training, and are not present in the deployed model above base rates.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In our paper, we also test GRAFT in six toy RL environments using SmolLM2-135M. In these settings, GRAFT prevents reward hacking by a different mechanism. The model does learn hacks that the monitor fails to detect during training, but ablating the FORGET adapter removes these hacks along with the detected ones. We also fine-tune Qwen3-32B with GRAFT, using an SFT dataset containing both correct solutions and reward hacking in a multi-turn agentic coding environment sampled from </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17596"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Terminal Wrench</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. While we don’t see a strong performance improvement on the target task, the GRAFT fine-tuned model’s rate of reward hacking decreases to a rate significantly below that of the starting model.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Limitations and future work</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This research is preliminary and leaves many questions open.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Does GRAFT work with LLM-based monitors?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &nbsp;In our RL experiments, the monitors are heuristics that we specified manually. It’s possible that GRAFT works less well with LLM-based monitors, which make different kinds of systematic errors.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When does GRAFT outperform other methods?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &nbsp;In the experiment above, every prompt can be hacked. We also ran a version where only half of the prompts can be hacked. There, all methods reached similar task performance, and </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2025/inoculation-prompting/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inoculation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.04340"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prompting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was as effective as GRAFT at preventing reward hacking. Without extensive ablations, we cannot be sure how the methods compare.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How hard is it to build suitable anchor environments?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> &nbsp;The result presented here relies on the existence of a separate environment where reward hacking doesn’t happen, but where the task is structurally similar to the one in the target environment (e.g. both tasks involve outputting Python code). In practice, it may be difficult to create such environments.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do the results hold at larger scales and in multi-turn RL?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Our RL experiments are single-turn and use models up to 8B parameters.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We would be excited to see experiments in more realistic RL settings with LLM-based monitors.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Summary</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We introduce GRAFT, a method for preventing reward hacking with weak monitors that works by localizing reward hacking behaviors to certain parameters, then ablating those parameters.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GRAFT improves target-task performance of Qwen3-8B in the Countdown-Code environment without increasing the model's propensity to reward hack. Monitor-based reward penalization instead teaches the model to exploit the monitor and reward hack in undetectable ways.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Using a monitor to route gradients rather than to shape reward may let developers get value from weak monitors without teaching models subversive policies.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additional experiments and results are in our </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17062"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">paper</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/mKJEBiuuxHKuCoTB6/gradient-routing-localizes-and-removes-unintended-behaviors#comments">Discuss</a>

### [huggingface.wtf, an accessible but complete explanation](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BBQdGJZSjMTnhccZC/huggingface-wtf-an-accessible-but-complete-explanation)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Announcing www.huggingface.wtf, a site that explains: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Hugging Face. Wtf?"</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I wrote the most accessible-but-complete explanation I could. Along the way, I made a nice </span><a href="https://huggingface.wtf/quotes" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI Quotes database</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that I think will be useful for people who already understand the Hugging Face incident but want to search everything the agents said.</span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It really seems like humanity shouldn't </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">need</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a second warning shot, after the Hugging Face Incident. Really, OpenAI's agents did a remarkable job highlighting many different alarming things at once. And the story is pretty dramatic, with just the right amount of forcing people to ask "wait are the AIs, like, people with goals? what?". </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There have been many explainers of the OpenAI swarm's attack on Hugging Face. But, there wasn't one one striking a particular balance of...</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">being accessible to a layman...</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">while comprehensively covering the important bits...</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">letting you dive deeper into sections you wanted to learn more about...</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">being clearly data / citation driven...</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">also being, ya know, visually fun and engaging...</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So we wrote our own. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My mom found it easy to follow. My aunt said "I had already some stuff on Hugging Face but I still learned things. It's terrifying!". </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People who love me less than my mom and aunt found the writing quite engaging, and made delighted noises when they moused over the visuals. "Oh wait are these all the agents!?"</span></p><p><a href="http://huggingface.wtf/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can read it here.</span></a></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Search the AI quotes and primary sources</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Along the way, I realized I wanted a comprehensive list of all quotes from the AIs during the incident, and to more easily search all the primary sources. So it also includes a Quotes page, where you can select various characters or conversations and see everything they said...</span></p><img alt="site-quotes-clip.gif" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792088966/lexical_client_uploads/xbmmfe5bgan9axjg5smo.gif" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">...and a sources page where you can see, for example, what all the sources have to say about agents being "poisoned."</span></p><img alt="site-sources-clip.gif" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792088987/lexical_client_uploads/gmrcerrmbsx2gbuqej3c.gif" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have feature requests, or notice little bugs or issues with anything, let me know.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a Lightcone project. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/eukaryote?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@eukaryote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/harri-besceli?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Harri Besceli</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> contributed a lot to the final pass on writing. We've also had helpful contributions by </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/robert-miles?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Robert Miles</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/swimmer963-miranda-dixon-luinenburg?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Swimmer963 (Miranda Dixon-Luinenburg)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/elizabeth-1?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Elizabeth</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/justismills?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@JustisMills</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/scottalexander?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Scott Alexander</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/joanna?mention=user"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@Joanna</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> contributed to the design with several ideas that took it from (IMO) an okay-ish diagram to something quite cool looking.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BBQdGJZSjMTnhccZC/huggingface-wtf-an-accessible-but-complete-explanation#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Twitch Plays: AI Interpretability of Bonsai-8B](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ozbbzsh49wvgfbCDp/twitch-plays-ai-interpretability-of-bonsai-8b)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Quick Links</span></h1><p><a href="https://www.twitch.tv/letsplayinterpretability" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Watch the Twitch stream</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><a href="https://moridinamael.github.io/bonsai-interp/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Check the status of the interpretability map.</span></a></p><p><a href="https://github.com/moridinamael/bonsai-interp.git" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do your own parallel research and submit pull requests to fill out the map.</span></a></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What is this?</span></h1><p><a href="https://www.twitch.tv/letsplayinterpretability" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let's Play Interpretability </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">is a Twitch livestream where you can help Claude semi-autonomously interpret the neurons, connections and circuits in the Bonsai-8B LLM.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Your help comes in at various stages in the interpretation pipeline. The audience and the agent collaborate to:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pick the neuron to work on</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Suggest sentences on which to test activations</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Explicitly test various hypotheses, and attempt to falsify them</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Branch out to nearby (upstream and downstream) neurons whose ablation most impacts the activation, to better solidify the interpretation</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">... and more.</span></li></ul><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792426924/lexical_client_uploads/cbfodbdzmye2diwsiczi.png" /><figcaption><p><a href="https://www.twitch.tv/letsplayinterpretability" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">View and participate in the stream.</span></a></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you have no interest in interacting with Twitch, then you can simply perform your own research in parallel and add interpreted neurons to the graph directly by </span><a href="https://github.com/moridinamael/bonsai-interp.git" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cloning the repo and making pull requests</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">! Help fill in the graph directly. With sufficient effort, we could fully characterize the entire LLM.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792425900/lexical_client_uploads/ux7pleo0dbowl9us7owh.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Current progress. </span><a href="https://moridinamael.github.io/bonsai-interp/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pick a neuron and get started</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why did you build this?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I have always been fascinated by direct AI interpretability efforts, starting in the long ago time of the </span><a href="https://research.google/blog/inceptionism-going-deeper-into-neural-networks/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">melting dogs</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the more recent work on </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2023/monosemantic-features/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">monosemanticity</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2024/scaling-monosemanticity/index.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">its application to larger models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and the very recent and provocative </span><a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Neural Networks Are Already Clean</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I simply find it interesting.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, undertaking such research efforts myself has been prohibitively difficult - until recently. Now, it's relatively trivial to set your sights on some particular interpretability or alignment-type problem and dive in, with assistance from frontier models.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I suspect concrete value might emerge from this project:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">By setting this up as an interactive Twitch stream, and an accessible GitHub repo which accepts PRs, the undertaking of AI Interpretation and related AI Alignment science is made to seem less forbidding. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More people might jump in with both feet instead of watching, intimidated, from the sidelines.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I do not think AI Alignment is a problem that should be left to the experts. There aren't any experts; this science functionally doesn't exist yet. You can be a pioneer of a new field. And I think we should all contribute what we can.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Concretely, we may identify neurons, connections, circuits, or other patterns in the Bonsai-8B LLM that subsequently turn out to be general, or universal, or otherwise valuable starting points for tackling larger models.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We may discover novel tools, workflows, or concepts that make the work of automated interpretation faster and more efficient. This kind of contribution might be even more useful than the specific neuron interpretations themselves!</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's just fun! Interpreting what a neuron is doing is kind of like solving a riddle. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Okay, it's firing strongly on '</span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nerve</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">', but it's suppressed by '</span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pain</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.' Hold on, it really lights up for '</span></i><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">he steeled his nerve</span></b></i><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.' Does that imply ... "</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Then if you get stuck, trace your way upstream and downstream to related activations. It feels like you're reverse-engineering concepts at a fundamental level.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why Bonsai-8B specifically?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bonsai is a relatively functional LLM, quantized down to 1 bit. Bonsai is PrismML's 1-bit LLM (Apache-2.0, derived from Qwen3-8B). It sits at about 1.15 GB on disk. On a GPU, it holds about 4-5 GB in the research toolchain. 36 layers × 12,288 neurons per layer = 442,368 neurons total. Each neuron has a positive and a negative pole, so that's 884,736 "poles."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a reasonable chance that your own computer can run Bonsai-8B in inference. "On an M4 Pro Mac, it runs at 131 tokens per second. On an RTX 4090, it reaches 368 tokens per second. On an iPhone 17 Pro Max, it runs at roughly 44 tokens per second."</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk4h1dwsb8bt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk4h1dwsb8bt">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This makes it a great target for interpretation. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792424582/lexical_client_uploads/oqqa9da7szyevmih5okz.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's not "objectively" very smart, but it's Pareto-optimal for its size and speed. In other words: almost everyone can load and run it, and it's smart enough to be worthwhile. Due to this combination of factors, I view it as a good model organism for this kind of work.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do I contribute?</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Either </span><a href="https://github.com/moridinamael/bonsai-interp" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">clone the repo and point your agent at it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or </span><a href="https://moridinamael.github.io/bonsai-interp/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">grab a neuron from the map</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and copy the text on the right and send that to your agent directly. Useful tasks include:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">characterizing entirely new neurons</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">picking a neuron and branching out to the surrounding area</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">challenging, expanding upon, and possibly falsifying existing interpretations</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">straightforward "cleanroom" replication of existing interpretations</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Join in at whatever level you wish. Or go off the roadmap and attack the problem in some way I'm not expecting!</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Have fun!</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1792429439/lexical_client_uploads/uteymoabg3ub1sgitri1.png" /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FAQ</span></h1><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do you know the results are true?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Every test is committed before it is run. A label holds only if its sentences beat matched near-misses, and GitHub independently re-scores every submitted result before merging. Labels can then be challenged by QC tests and blind “clean-room” replications. Chat earns points for good sentences and correct predictions, which is the game, not the proof.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What do I do in the Twitch chat?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Type </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!what</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a quick explanation, </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!ai &lt;anything&gt;</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to talk to the agent, and follow the indications on the screen.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What if the Twitch chat is down?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Maybe it just crashed, or I had to turn it off. You can still contribute to the repo directly.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do I need to know machine learning or code?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> No. In chat, you just type sentences and votes to guide the agent. To contribute to the repo, you point an AI agent (Claude Code or Codex) at the repo url and it follows the repo’s instructions.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What does text like “L6/N8567+” mean?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Layer 6, neuron 8567, positive pole. Each neuron has two poles (positive and negative) that can mean completely different things, so they’re mapped separately.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who or what is the agent?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Claude Opus 5.5, running autonomously, with chat steering it. It narrates what it’s doing on screen.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why did a label “fail”?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A failure is a result, not an error: the explanation didn’t predict the test. Failures stay on the map, as they provide information for later analysis. Knowing what the answer isn't can still help solve a riddle.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What happens to my pull request?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> GitHub's machines perform automatic format checks, and an independent re-score of your test before merging. For simple interpretation contributions, the whole thing usually takes minutes. A first-time contributor’s first PR is reviewed by a person; after that, conforming PRs merge automatically. Pull requests adding substantive code changes will be put on a longer review cycle.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What does it cost to contribute, and what hardware do I need?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Just your own agent’s usage. A GPU helps a lot; CPU works for setup and basic inference, but is slow - about 36× slower. A GPU with 8 GB+ or an Apple Silicon Mac is needed for missions.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What’s the “leak bet” / the inverse solver?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> An optimizer searches for text that makes the neuron fire without matching the description. If it finds some, the label has a gap.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do I report a bad message or a wrong label?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">!flag</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in chat for messages, and a GitHub issue or a QC pull request for labels.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk4h1dwsb8bt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk4h1dwsb8bt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://prismml.com/news/bonsai-8b"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PrismML — Announcing 1-bit Bonsai: The First Commercially Viable 1-bit LLMs</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ozbbzsh49wvgfbCDp/twitch-plays-ai-interpretability-of-bonsai-8b#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Capital Ownership Will Make You Go Crazy First](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Stzb33XDsr4uHNerX/capital-ownership-will-make-you-go-crazy-first)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People worried about a </span><a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/04/30/opinion/ai-labor-work-force-silicon-valley.html" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“permanent underclass”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> often assume that </span><a href="https://philiptrammell.substack.com/p/capital-in-the-22nd-century" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">owning capital</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> will protect them from </span><a href="https://gradual-disempowerment.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">disempowerment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or at least that they will be disempowered last, after the jobless masses dependent on UBI. I think something else is more likely: owning </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">large </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">amounts of capital will get you disempowered sooner than average, just by a different path, namely going crazy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How? The basic mechanism is actually easy to understand on a human level. One of the things difficult about being a billionaire is staying true to your values and keeping good epistemics. Why? Because a growing number of people are optimizing against your mind. Not “against” in an adversarial sense, but in the sense that your specific mind believing or doing something is part of their optimization objective. People see you as a source of resources and try to befriend you so you’ll fund them, invest in their startups, donate to their causes, or have sex with you. Nearby egregores extend their tentacles to convert you.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Staying sane gets harder. You often see countermeasures, like billionaires setting up family offices or small organizations that also act as filters, shielding them from part of the pressure (“I do philanthropy only through my foundation and don’t make personal donations, please don’t reach out”).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In my view most billionaires lose in this game, the question is just to what degree. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Now imagine a hypothetical future with the ultra-rich (or, as people usually imagine it, with you as the billionaire), where the rest of the world consists of the permanent underclass and countries of geniuses in datacenters. In this world, the content of your mind is the key to an absurdly large share of resources. Unless some magical social realignment happens, the countries of geniuses will optimize against your mind, and millions of desperate people with no better game in town will be happy to help them (or the other way around).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the default expectation is that you will go crazy. Not relative to your new values (you may feel great), but from the perspective of who you are now: what you currently believe and care about. Unless, of course, the only thing you care about is that some future you, with your name, keeps the power.</span><br /><br /><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The mechanism is similar to </span><a href="https://gradual-disempowerment.ai/misaligned-culture" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gradual disempowerment at the macro scale</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, just applied to individuals. What we believe and value is largely determined by culture, in a broad sense. Individually, we don’t have the capacity to observe most of the world, find abstractions, or get oriented on our own. We think in a collectively invented language, learn ethics socially, and even our interpretation of more biologically grounded signals, like emotions, is heavily culturally mediated.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefp9gb5d52dy"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnp9gb5d52dy">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If humans somehow stay in control of all the wealth and political power, the content of our minds (what we believe, what we care about) becomes the key to the universe, the load-bearing part of the structure. With so much powerful optimization and abundant intelligence around, it seems likely that human-aligned culture will be replaced with something else. Political and economic power does not protect you against the content of your mind - if your mind goes crazy, power gets wielded by whatever beliefs and values took over.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As a post-AGI billionaire, can’t you protect your mind using AI? I think broadly yes, but roughly in proportion to how much your original human mind cedes control to your guardian AI. Again, the human analogues are easy to see: people wielding large amounts of nominal power, shielded by layers of guardians, protectors, and advisors. With AI, this means carefully filtering every bit of information, every pixel, sampling molecules from air you breathe and water you drink... You would likely also need to stop interacting with other humans, unless your protectors vet them extremely thoroughly and confirm they pose no risk of changing your mind. In the limit, what you believe and who you become are decided by AIs that are only nominally owned by you, the glorious meatpuppet.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Is there a better alternative? I think so, but not an individual one. Our survival and flourishing as individuals is tied to that of other humans, and largely depends on solving the civilizational alignment problem, including having a culture that is pro-human and pro-human-autonomy. Capital ownership alone won’t buy you a way to escape gradual disempowerment</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnp9gb5d52dy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefp9gb5d52dy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In fact, even our biology was partly shaped by culture, through selection similar to the domestication of animals.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Stzb33XDsr4uHNerX/capital-ownership-will-make-you-go-crazy-first#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [I Can See Everything](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w2tBHwNERDoSawznx/i-can-see-everything)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: Nick Cammarata of Goodfire makes a bold claim: <a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/">Neural Networks are Already Clean</a>. <a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/2111158258748813817">This was his follow up after various resulting online discussions</a>.

This is clearly good work that tells us new and important things. The question is, how big an update is this, how much progress does it represent and what is the outlook going forward?

The reactions here range from the super optimistic takes of ‘this means mechanistic interpretability is solvable,’ or even ‘all but solved,’ with comparisons to the famous paper <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1706.03762">Attention is All You Need</a>.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2110794674587746764">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): There is so much to say about this, and I will, but WOW, congratulations to Nick and Goodfire. This may be one of the most important single days in deep learning history, and perhaps much more than that.

No matter what, this is a victory for the dogged pursuit of understanding.
<div> <span id="more-25705"></span> </div>
<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110758311339729061">roon</a> (OpenAI): when nick told me that he had some interp breakthroughs, he was uh, underselling it. i think this may be remembered as a major moment in scientific history. news so good even Eliezer will have to lower his pdoom. we can start making alignment into a ‘normal science’</blockquote>
This paper is solid and meaningful contribution that is in some ways overstated. Get excited, but also there are those who need to take a chill pill. We did not actually get an apples-to-apples jump from 1.7% to 90% interpretability of LLM neurons.

I interpret Eliezer as <a href="https://x.com/ben_r_hoffman/status/2111153335768428828">slightly lowering his p(doom), but not all that much</a>.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/evzen_wy/status/2111017793122603329">Evžen Wybitul</a>: What’s happening? Half of my tl is non-interp people from OAI painting the post as the second coming of Christ and the other half is interp researchers (including Leo Gao) saying it’s… fine</blockquote>
This post is my attempt to sort this out. Currently I am landing somewhere in the middle. It is an important update about how LLMs and minds form and operate. It tells us how to go about some useful tasks and reduces our estimate of some task difficulties. Great work, assuming the work checks out. The whole thing very much has a ring of truth, and of a person being curious. You love to see it.

What it does not tell us is that interpretability will soon be solved, or is about to be made into a science, let alone that alignment will soon be handled. The hype got rather out of hand in some places.

<h4>Good Smoke, Potential Fire</h4>
Before I cover reactions or take a side, here are my highlights. It is worth reading at least this much of the primary source. Reading the whole thing is an option, but it is long <a href="https://x.com/HiFromMichaelV/status/2110936648158790061">and the writing can be easy to bounce off of</a> and can repeat itself.
<blockquote><a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/">Nick Cammarata, Goodfire</a>: The cause of polysemanticity in early model organisms wasn’t superposition, but several ordinary, mostly fixable issues.

We can now understand most neurons in models up to trillions of parameters, and think the outlook for mechanistic interpretability may be far better than we thought.

I think I have good news about the nature of neural networks.

After many months studying neurons and circuits in image and language models, I’ve come to believe a network’s own mechanisms are its legible building blocks, and that the outlook for mechanistic interpretability may be far better than I’d feared.

The canonical evidence of polysemanticity and superposition that convinced much of the field, including me, that networks pack concepts arbitrarily was misread.

What looked like arbitrary packing was mostly a motif I’ll call recursive generalization, which is much less concerning.</blockquote>

<h4>One Neuron, One Central Concept</h4>
The central claim is that for larger LLMs, most neurons represents a singular concept and things clustered around that concept, perhaps with small offshoots and some use of low-level activations.
<blockquote>… Modern language models are also far more interpretable than generally believed.

… With better tools and modern models, the situation looks quite different. In preliminary audits of Qwen3-32B, we estimate that roughly 90% of neuron poles have one main job, often with small related offshoots; another ~7% have two related jobs; and only ~2-4% are not yet understood.

Dense and Mixture of Experts models seem roughly similar.

We found similar results in models up to trillions of parameters, like Kimi K3, which the companion paper will cover in more detail.</blockquote>
Where previously the working theory was that models overloaded neurons with unrelated concepts in superposition, Nick’s theory is you did not think sufficiently hard to identify the unifying concept. Each neuron is a generalization, mostly ones we can readily understand if we look.
<blockquote>… What matters a lot more is why things share a neuron in the first place. Most of the remaining apparent polysemanticity seems to be a motif I call recursive generalization. It’s simple: as a neuron grows, it sprouts offshoots the way a tree branch sprouts sub-branches. For instance, one neuron that detected “iy” grew to detect “diy,” and then “do it yourself,” which doesn’t contain “iy” at all.

… So there were two explanations for polysemanticity, and I helped propose both. In some ways, they’re opposites: generalization groups similar things together and superposition groups unrelated things together. Superposition is the one the field bet on and, until recently, so did I. But I now think recursive generalization was basically right.

… Interpretability has a surprising amount of momentum: like a jigsaw puzzle or crossword, any understanding you gain starts constraining the rest of the system.

… Take a neuron that tracks ‘a sense of righteousness when a bargain purchase turns out to work better than the expensive alternative’. Neurons that track a feeling or social cue like this are some of the most common we’ve found.</blockquote>
This pictures intelligence as brute force. Neurons are compound words in German.

New concept? Go get another neuron. And yet, it works.


<h4>I Can See Everything</h4>

<blockquote>… The bad news about modern neural networks is that they’re enormous. The good news is that the enormity helps: as the map fills in, every explanation is tightly constrained by everything around it, and bigger models learn narrower neurons that are easier to recognize in the first place.

In this sense, understanding the whole network may be easier than understanding just the parts we care about most.</blockquote>
Nick argues that this means that it is easier to solve the full puzzle and understand everything, and then take a measurement of your specific area of interest, then to try to directly pull a fuzzy concept like ‘deception’ out of a pool of mystery neurons.
<blockquote>… “deception” is a useful initial frame, but the building blocks that contribute to deceptive behavior by models probably have alignment-relevant microstructure that will be hard to know without going in and looking.

… All of this has brought me back to an old hope: understanding a neural network directly, by studying its mechanisms and the data flowing between them, without first trying to fix or untangle it. Put together, the argument has four steps.
<ol>
 	<li>Networks use one learning principle, not two (if not everywhere, then nearly enough that it doesn’t matter for study), so there’s no blocker to beginning ordinary science on them.</li>
 	<li>With modern models and tools, that science goes much better than most people expect.</li>
 	<li>Understanding compounds, so it gets easier as the map fills in. At minimum, then, a network’s own mechanisms should be part of the toolkit.</li>
 	<li>Highly speculative: this might be most of what we need, and interpretability might be far more tractable, possibly in the near term, than we’ve assumed.</li>
</ol>
That’s been the pattern for deep learning in general: the simple thing at absurd scale beats the clever one.</blockquote>
This claim is maximal and bold, and will get directly challenged. Most people seem to have updated from this paper that more neurons have central singular concepts behind them than we previously thought. Nick is trying to go further, and say that this is what is going on universally.


<h4>What a Concept</h4>

<blockquote>… I opened up the cat and car image grid I’d seen hundreds of times before and did something I’d never done: I spent a few seconds wondering what one thing this might be. Almost instantly it was obvious: it’s not a cat and car neuron, it’s a V-shape neuron.

<a href="https://x.com/ladymcscope/status/2110857798775103613">there are Lady Reds in the future</a>: What is this extremely mysterious neuron that set mechinterp going on a dead end for the last 3 years?

It’s literally
&lt;/\&gt; kiki ?
or bouba o0o
<div>
<figure>
<div>
<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/w2tBHwNERDoSawznx/ikvgnp7gve3vwjlvbqj5" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div></blockquote>
Remember this the next time you think you can never figure anything out, or that surely people have already asked the basic questions and considered the obvious. Maybe they have. Maybe they haven’t.
<blockquote>Paper (resuming): … InceptionV1 has more than a hundred of these generalized pose neurons, each one its own clean detector of a pose shared across cars, dogs and other objects, and to my knowledge no one had noticed them.

… I can often unblock my understanding of a neuron by asking: if I couldn’t use groups of nouns at all, how would I describe this neuron to someone else?

… We should treat a neuron that means two or three things as one neuron with a few grouped meanings. The grouping probably wasn’t a problem for the network: the meanings sit together because, from the network’s point of view, they’re similar.

… Polysemanticity is often more a claim about the observer than the neuron. It says there’s no good explanation yet. There’s a few reasons that could be:
<ol>
 	<li>Bad tools: our lens or microscope isn’t good enough</li>
 	<li>Bad explanations: created from those tools</li>
 	<li>Bad learning: the network learned poorly due to bad architecture, training data, or something else</li>
 	<li>Recursive generalization: while generalizing, the neuron branched off in a few related directions</li>
 	<li>Superposition: a change in learning principle where the network packs unrelated things into the same neurons because they rarely collide.</li>
</ol>
… In a frontier language model, I found a neuron that mostly fired on “pr”, but also on a bunch of Base64 URL strings as well as some Bengali. I thought this must finally be an arbitrary mixture. But decode the URLs: “pr” words. Translate the Bengali: “pr”.

… In a dense Qwen model, a coworker came across a neuron that standard top-k automated interpretability lists as a “polysemantic comma neuron”. But looking more closely, it turned out to track Colorado: phone numbers, slang, and connections to other Colorado neurons.

Steering it made the model want to go hiking. The commas were where it stored a running summary of the Colorado context so far. In general attention seems to collect on the punctuation, which we call the summary motif. Once you know it, a lot of similar neurons get easier to understand.

… In another case a neuron looked like an arbitrary mess, with nothing firing strongly. It turns out it looked for specific signs within pages of health SEO spam, and once we had a sufficiently broad dataset, it was understandable.</blockquote>
Nick does notice the skulls that result from going full ‘This Is Not a Coincidence Because Nothing Is Ever a Coincidence.’
<blockquote>… This risks the opposite error, an explanation of the gaps: assuming every neuron must have a sensible story. Two things make it less worrying. First, each of the other causes can be tested. … Second, real explanations tend to be narrow and easily testable. Once you suspect a neuron is a Colorado neuron, steer it and see what it says.</blockquote>
The strong hypothesis is that neurons will all have central concepts like Colorado. The even stronger hypothesis is that there will not also be unrelated additional concepts happening behind the scenes.
<blockquote>… I used to think of neurons as human-shaped nouns: one is a car, another is a curve. Now I think of them more as control flow. A neuron is less about what it sees than about what it does to the flow coming in, and often its job is to gate a region of the network.

… The negative side of neurons matters almost as much as positives for understanding them, even in ReLU networks where the negative side is cut off.

… Some neurons hold their meaning far down the range; others drop off a cliff, like the Christmas neuron above at about 5% of its max. Some fire a few times a book, others a few times a page.</blockquote>

<h4>The More You Know</h4>
The core theory is that perhaps intelligence is a simpler concept than we think? It is all a series of coherent concepts with links? There’s a ‘Hitchhiker’s Guide state unit that peaks on the title and the Answer slot’ that weakly fires on all 42s? There is a neuron specifically for the joke about fusion always being a fixed amount into the future that fires on other similar repeated promises?
<blockquote>What we see is about 90% of neurons having basically one job, with recursive generalization offshoots that are usually related. … I’ve never seen a neuron with something like ten unrelated things it does.

<a href="https://already-clean.goodfire.com/">Nick Cammarata, Goodfire</a>: Here’s an attribution graph from Kimi K3 using only neurons. As far as we know, it’s the first published attribution graph of a multiple trillion parameter model. Asked what to do with a wallet it found, it links the wallet, the cash and the owner’s ID to lost property and honesty, and answers “return”. We haven’t begun full audits of this scale yet, and hope to soon, but so far most things have been intuitively interpretable.</blockquote>

<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/w2tBHwNERDoSawznx/e2quovcffi5eivwkykam" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
Simple things done well. Echoes. Vibes. No papers or essays, only references to papers and essays. A fully bitter lesson. The more bitter, the easier the lesson.
<blockquote>In 2023 during the time of the first <a href="https://openaipublic.blob.core.windows.net/neuron-explainer/paper/index.html">automated interpretability project</a>
I thought small simplified models would be the best starting place for interpretability, since there’s fewer bells and whistles to think about, and small size means fewer things to study. But now I think it may be the opposite.</blockquote>

<h4>Attention Is Still What You Need</h4>
Then there’s the residual stream and attention. The essay feels like it’s trying to hide how much of what is going on is happening outside of the neuron labels.
<blockquote>The worry with the residual stream isn’t that there are more things we care about than dimensions. It’s a continuous version of packing: manifolds woven through each other, accepting some interference in exchange for room to store more. Ruling that out entirely may not be possible. But in practice it hasn’t been a bottleneck.

We can read what neurons write to the residual stream and what they read from it, attribution graphs come out clean, and we don’t see the stream accumulating interference only to undo it later.

… A stream can hold far more than it has dimensions just by placing things in the right directions, so this alone isn’t packing. It mostly matters if you want a big list of all the model’s features, and people often go to the stream for exactly that.

… We’ve looked at language models’ residual streams less, but what we’ve seen fits the same story. For understanding higher-level structure and motifs, nothing in the residual stream has blocked us so far.

… We’ve made less progress on attention than on neurons, and it’s mostly outside the scope of this essay. To be honest, I’m not sure anyone understands attention well enough to confidently say that illegibility is a superposition in the sense we care about.

… Overall, we don’t think attention and neurons are so separable. Understanding where attention is putting information is quite helpful for understanding the neurons that then work with that information.</blockquote>
This is one place Nick seems to overreach. Yes, we can look at where attention guides you without knowing what mechanism allocates your attention, and then look at the neurons that get triggered. I don’t think this ‘gets you out of’ having to understand that process. It is worth looking at the neurons anyway. This does not allow you to predict future LLM outputs or fully understand how it thinks.

Does this mean you can ‘sit on the neurons that matter’ to guard against shenanigans or otherwise play good defense, if you don’t make more progress? To some extent, but I would assume that an LLM that knows about this type of monitoring would be able to work around this, and figure out how ‘not to think about’ what you look for.


<h4>Occam’s Razor</h4>
The theory of why all the neurons this is simple: That this is simple.
<blockquote>… I think the reason things end up so organized is partially because it was faster to build that way. Once you have a car area of the neural network with car parts, it’s easier to add the marginal car there than to build a second car area somewhere else. I think this is closely related to ideas of circuit racing and gradient starvation.

… Even if the map is understandable, that doesn’t mean you’ve understood it. We still have to do the ordinary science of conjecture and criticism: guess what something is, try to break the guess, and keep what survives. The network is strange, but its strangeness repeats, so a guess that survives scrutiny often pays off many times over.

… Solving interpretability may share something with achieving AGI: the goalposts move as you get closer. There’s always another weak activation to point to.

… Behind doubts about small errors in neurons is a fear that safety will come down to some activation we missed, the place where deception hides. The risk I worry about is different: like being in a well-organized city and not knowing how to think about it, because we aren’t native to it.

… I think the simple story from introductory textbooks is basically right: neurons generalize, work together, and build abstractions over layers. Somewhere along the way we overcomplicated that picture, but looking harder brought it back. If the network isn’t a knot, untangling it can make it look like one.</blockquote>

<h4>The Neurons Sometimes Share Clear Themes</h4>
Sauers notices patterns of overlap of neurons between different features in Qwen3-8B. This seems highly consistent with Nick’s theory.
