<h4>Hype</h4>

<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110949943175496171">roon</a>: I will say that even in bona fide a scientific revolution, you wouldn’t expect the fields previous experts to be the first convinced. in fact, they may be the last

<a href="https://x.com/LakeSnyde/status/2110952554423296381">Lake Snyder</a>: It’s an interesting dichotomy that Roon is completely convinced but the (slightly) older guard is skeptical.

<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110957427533513061">roon</a>: I honestly think that being in mechinterp pre this paper may have been an infohazard if what he is saying is true

<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110758312853832083">roon</a>: I feel like my understanding of what a neural net is has been catapulted into the future by five years or something. it has brought back a lot of the sheer joy I had for studying the mystery of deep learning. neurons are already clean! they form a wonderful alien ontology!</blockquote>
We saw some hype up top. Here is some more hype.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2110765687891489058">Andrew Curran</a>: This would be a massive positive update for me on the future. I had long since given up on reaching any real level of interpretability before superintelligence arrived. I stopped writing about it because of how demoralizing it was. Hard to overstate how big this may be.

… To help people understand how far away this seemed, this is Dario in April of last year – in what at the time was a huge positive update on this subject – saying we would reach it within 5-10 years. On many timelines now, including my own, this would have been too late.

Dario Amodei (April 2025): On one hand, recent progress—especially the results on circuits and on interpretability-based testing of models—has made me feel that we are on the verge of cracking interpretability in a big way. Although the task ahead of us is Herculean, I can see a realistic path towards interpretability being a sophisticated and reliable way to diagnose problems in even very advanced AI—a true “MRI for AI”. In fact, on its current trajectory I would bet strongly in favor of interpretability reaching this point within 5-10 years.</blockquote>
This is exactly the mistake Eliezer Yudkowsky will be ranting about later in this post. Yes, this is progress. No, this is not an ‘MRI for AI.’ That is still a long way off.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/eric_ho/status/2110759126599094458">Eric Ho</a> (Goodfire): it’s been an enormous privilege and joy to watch @nickcammarata cook this up over the last year. week after week, experiment after experiment, he unraveled the tangled mess of our thinking about neural nets to reveal the truth.

i never knew quite what to expect on our weekly walks. each conversation brought a torrent of new theories about the nature of AI, some conjectures teetering on the brink of madness, and others that seemed to cut through years of confusion with a single sentence.

nick is one of the great scientists of our time. his intuition and research taste are out of this world, but what sets him apart is his unmatched ability to capture the essence of what something truly is, without being lost in the details. i would highly recommend you read his entire essay if you’re curious about AI. it’s long, but written beautifully.

nick’s research makes me far more optimistic about the future of AI alignment. i hope that it can inspire the same for you.

<a href="https://x.com/GoodfireAI/status/2110758907895484731">Goodfire</a>: For years, neural networks looked inscrutable, like tangled knots that needed to be unwound. We no longer think that’s the case. AI models are far more interpretable than we thought. We think this is great news for interpretability.

Goodfire’s @nickcammarata co-authored Zoom In, the paper that introduced polysemanticity and superposition. He spent months revisiting the vision model organisms that much of interpretability grew out of and studying modern language models up to trillions of parameters. His new essay explains what the early field, him included, got wrong, why there’s reason for optimism today, and why there might be a bitter lesson for interpretability.</blockquote>
Even if you think this is a big deal, <a href="https://x.com/MajmudarAdam/status/2110831397321740387">some of the hype got crazy</a>.
<blockquote>Adam Majmudar (OpenAI): Yea this is the most important result of the year, at the level of significance of “Attention Is All You Need”. It brings us from “is alignment even solvable” to “we may solve alignment in the next year.” And finally unlocks true recursive self-alignment enabled by RSI.

1/ neural networks are not fundamentally unintelligible to humans

2/ neural networks are fundamentally clean

3/ the language of humans and the language of neural networks is the same

4/ alignment can progress as fast as RSI. recursive self-improvement unlocks recursive self-alignment and a scaling law for alignment.

<a href="https://x.com/MajmudarAdam/status/2110924292997476722">adammaj</a>: I def don’t think this is an argument for RSI or that alignment is solved. It’s just a reason for optimism in an otherwise bleak landscape of the rate of progress, which is what I intended to express

<a href="https://x.com/MajmudarAdam/status/2110852749097374018">adammaj</a>: Lemme add some VERY important nuance after reading some comments:
&gt; When I say “we may solve alignment in the next year” I should have said “we may solve controllability” – I don’t mean the whole alignment problem. Values is a completely different problem. Also 1 year is ambitious. Maybe 2-4 years is more realistic. But you have to price in the rapid increases in rate of progress which keeps beating human expectations.
&gt; Comparing it to “attention is all you need” is a pretty aggressive comparison, and I mean it not today but in the limit (ie. if everything Nick says here is true and the research direction continues to play out as expected – let’s see when they release their technical paper). Probably pretty stupid to say conclusively now, should be read more like a prediction about the impact this direction could have.
&gt; Solving interp from here will still be hard and is a very large/complex problem. I am no interp expert personally but there are good interp people in the comments adding nuance. But this seems like a very big update that this method is much more promising then expected and might work out.

<a href="https://x.com/siddarthv66/status/2110915429585608938">Siddarth</a>: im sorry wtf i feel like im going insane. This is a cool blog and exciting interp stuff (good job Nick!) but i think it’s very suspicious we have a bunch of openai people hyping it up as the greatest AI advancement this decade. I think I know why this is happening but I don’t want to speculate on the timeline too much. But in general, please update your priors responsibly people — alignment (and even interp) is FAR from solved. This is not the ok signal to accelerate RSI tomorrow</blockquote>
I too find it alarming that anyone could interpret this as ‘alignment can progress as fast as RSI’ levels of valuable. There was a remarkable eagerness of many, especially at OpenAI, to take it to 11 with how much this lets us pretend everything is fine. Even with the pullbacks we did see afterwards, wow.

I think mostly this is people desperate for a source of hope. You need those in life:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2110826347723722966">Dean W. Ball</a>: My son took his first independent steps today. Between that and the interpretability stuff I’m really overwhelmed with hope, which I have not been in some time.</blockquote>
You also need a story that lets you keep advancing while thinking it is all going to work out. This all pattern matches very closely to continuing the cycle of ‘there I fixed it, we can get back to training the models now’ until you have something else to patch over and pretend is fine.

It is hard not to look at reactions <a href="https://x.com/lmcorrigan1/status/2110779752021885316">and memes like this</a>, and get the feel of the underlying argument (not explicitly claimed at the link) of ‘those fools thought we were doomed and alignment was unsolvable because superposition, but now that we know there is less superposition, those people are wrong, which means we will solve alignment.’ No.

At first, the hype gave many <a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2110844861482242342">the same impression as OpenAI’s Drew Pusateri,</a> that all the AI people were super excited and the media was once again ignoring a huge story, and Kelsey Piper had to point out that media coverage takes time. Since then, I think a lot of people have settled into a more modest position.


<h4>Anti-Hype</h4>
I think Joshua is modestly underselling this paper in terms of how much it changes parts of our understanding and option space, but even he is overselling how much this implies about reliability of prosaic safety techniques, and also is underselling the propensity for the world to apply optimization pressure.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2110852879791886480">Joshua Achiam</a>: I think this is great and very cool! I am excited to see the insights here translate into tools with quantifiable success rates for helping us identify potential harms or steer neural networks away from risk.

A lot of people on the timeline today seem to be updating a lot over this – my own personal update is very small. This feels like one of those long-sequence-of-small-but-helpful-improvements types of papers, not something that should radically reshape our worldviews. It’s a good argument and one that could plausibly begin to constitute a reasonable prior. If correct it means that a lot of somewhat more “normal” or prosaic safety techniques will be reasonably reliable unless or until optimization pressure is applied against them.

It is however in principle possible that optimization pressure will be applied to design alien intelligences impenetrable by conventional interpretability techniques, and that these will be used against us by someone or something at some time.

There’s a picture I have for the future – an ecosystem of many different powerful AI systems, most of which are pretty good and pretty aligned and pretty helpful and pretty honest, some of which are rogue or self-sovereign, and a small number of which are actively malicious to human interests.

What the harmful ones will be able to accomplish will be limited, but not perfectly, by the good ones. Developments like good interp tools will help ensure that the good ones stay mostly good. But that there *will* exist good AIs that stay mostly good was already priced in for me, so this isn’t a huge update.</blockquote>
I have a lot of disagreements with the rest of the vision here but that’s another story.

Roko cautions that labeling neurons is like writing comments in code. <a href="https://x.com/RokoMijic/status/2110819919373701495">Some will be inaccurate, so you need whatever you do to survive that</a>. This undersells the longer term difficulties, since you will face optimization pressure, not only errors, but yes.


<h4>Theme and Variations</h4>
<a href="https://x.com/livgorton/status/2110799624667795624">Liv Gorton argues that Nick misunderstands superposition</a>. Superposition is, to Liv, a long-standing, well-defined claim that there are more features in a layer than dimension, via almost orthogonal directions, which is still true. There’s still plenty of complexity here. <a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/2110811157691076825">Nick responds that he suspects the difference</a> is that he doesn’t think it is useful to work with ‘features.’
<blockquote>Nick Cammarata: i found myself in situations where i feel like i understood the network pretty well, what was happening was generally simple in a given region, and most of the struggle was trying to convert it to a hypothetical feature ontology with no way to measure it. all the confusion was going into the wrong place, i just wanted to see how the mechanisms were moving data, which kept forming reliable motifs with clear explanations with predictions that held

what actually matters to me is the motif in the car-&gt;dogs circuit i found with chris.</blockquote>
More good discussion follows there. I do think Nick was likely overclaiming in places.

My understanding of Nick’s theory <a href="https://x.com/dcolascione/status/2111127873952116850">matches Daniel Colascione</a>. Each neuron is initiated early on in training so that it strongly activates on one clear, coherent concept. You want to focus on those concepts. Then weak activations build up around things related to the core concept in various ways.

<a href="https://x.com/sksq96/status/2110770268679410136">The claim is that</a> ~90% of neuron poles have such a primary activation, or 97% for stronger activations. About 81% are claimed to be clean on both sides.

This theory says that Nick is not denying superposition, but there should usually be some way to tie weak activations back to the central concept. Any given neuron, Nick is claiming, isn’t doing five unrelated overloaded things at once.

Thus, if there are two unrelated things going on with a neuron, and it fires equally on both, this creates a problem, especially since it is on Nick’s own example:
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/RatOrthodox/status/2110976835089809678">Brangus</a>: A Qwen3 neuron that tracks Colorado stuff was given as an example of a neuron that seems polysemantic but makes sense once you trace it. I had Claude check what else it tracks: Tornado’s Python HTTP client code, just as hard. Can’t wait to learn how Colorado and Tornado connect!

And it didn’t fire on all code btw. The same snippet written with aiohttp did not cause the neuron to fire basically at all. Same snippet with an HTTPClient from a fake library, still basically 0. It specifically tracks “This is Tornado.”

This weirdness does leak into answers btw. If you force Qwen to pick a US state that it most associates with Tornado, it assigns 59% to Colorado. If you zero out this neuron, that drops to 15%. The Claudes don’t seem to mention Colorado at all, but I’m curious about other models.

Nick’s essay never actually said the neuron only tracks Colorado stuff, but I do think this is a pretty weird second thing for the neuron to track! Doesn’t seem very clean to me.

This cost me about 6 bucks and 15 minutes btw. Feel free to check for yourself!

Here are the other things claude found btw.

Claude actually already thought of your “ends in ‘ado'” objection and tested it. Here are the results! [no pattern] Also btw, qwen3 does not tokenize “Colorado” or “tornado” or “Tornado” the way you think it might.</blockquote>
<div>

<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/w2tBHwNERDoSawznx/n348v4o8ajn2cwifwlth" /></figure>

<div></div>
</div></figure>
</div>
<a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gISEekxuEgk&amp;pp=ygUqaSd2ZSBnb3QgYSB0aGVvcnkgYnVmZnkgdGhlIHZhbXBpcmUgc2xheWVy">Roon’s got a theory</a>. You can play Six Degrees of Neuron Activation, but it turned out to be wrong.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110979088391585926">roon</a>: I have a theory and you’re not going to like it

… ok fuck it running the test is hard so let me just say the theory: tornado -&gt; Apache license -&gt; apaches -&gt; American southwest -&gt; Colorado

<a href="https://x.com/RatOrthodox/status/2110991637518381561">Brangus</a>: is it hard to run the experiment because you have to use Astra? :P
(jk, I love astra. Is good model.)

Honestly kinda.

I tested this and it’s wrong. the neuron really just loves the tornado web framework.</blockquote>
We could start by accepting that neurons often have secondary weak activations that have no clear link back to the core concept.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2111151636429341163">roon</a>: this is definitely “polysemantic”. it’s important to note this so critics don’t accuse you of drawing schizophrenic connections to support any theory. but if you read Nick’s paper he claims that some qwen neurons have a second unrelated smaller peak; that is still a much better interp world than we lived in last week, where we thought neurons could be an arbitrary mismash of superposed concepts. hell even if there are 3 or 5 we can read the stack of meanings like Lyra’s alethiometer

few interesting things about this neuron:
– it really likes the Tornado web framework but doesn’t care about tornadoes
– it responds weakly even to fictional software frameworks called Tornado
– it gets somewhat less excited by arbitrary web frameworks
– there is no connection to Ipython, which was invented in Colorado
– there is no connection to Apache, a software license but also a tribe that lives in the American southwest
– -ado connection doesn’t exist, “el dorado” does nothing

<a href="https://x.com/alethiawiz/status/2111154722593984521">alethiawiz</a>: it’s best to just wait for nick to release a technical paper.

<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2111155554207011184">roon</a>: No it’s best to start your own research program.

<a href="https://x.com/alethiawiz/status/2111157573269557418">alethiawiz</a>: you’re right.</blockquote>
The problem is that in this case, Brangus is saying the Colorado neuron fires ‘equally strongly’ on Tornado activations. And there is quite a lot of leakage between the concepts. If this is a typical result, then the whole paper won’t hold up that well.

The neuron meant as an example of a centrally explained neuron has at least two central explanations. That is a very bad sign.


<h4>Is This How Minds Work?</h4>
Almost everyone, including Eliezer Yudkowsky (who posted in a bunch of different threads, and whose comments I am moving into what I consider a more logical order), thinks this is a non-zero amount of Actual Progress, if the central findings hold up.

One objection is that some think the paper will not hold up.

The other objection is, even if the paper holds up, this is merely a beginning that makes it easier but still difficult to move forward.

The paper’s central claim is not to have found something new, but rather to have found the absence of things both old and new. The AI’s neurons look simpler, not more complicated. This leaves the rest of its behavior still unexplained.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2110837392508854388">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Right now they’re at the stage of ‘explaining where Coca-Cola comes from or how it gets made’ of… tracing how a quarter falls through a Coke vending machine before the Coke falls out.

Noticing that the AI is thinking about Navier-Stokes is very different from understanding the part where the AI can prove Navier-Stokes, and no human-written program can do so, because no human knows what kind of thought processes would be required.

<a href="https://manifold.markets/EliezerYudkowsky/by-the-end-of-2026-will-we-have-tra">See this Manifold market</a> [which is still at 8%, asking whether we will have transparency into any useful internal pattern within a Large Language Model whose semantics would have been unfamiliar to AI and cognitive science in 2006, which was persistently over 50% back in 2024.]

Eliezer Yudkowsky (comment on the manifold market from 2023): This market is not about identifying semantics inside an LLM. It is not about identifying semantics inside an LLM which we did not previously know to be inside LLMs. This market is about identifying semantics inside an LLM such that those semantics, once uncovered, teach us something about semantic representations in general which we did not know in 2006.</blockquote>

<h4>But We Can Open The Box, Said Toad</h4>
There is another key point Eliezer Yudkowsky emphasizes: If you have a ‘trying to do math’ detector via this new technique, that can be useful, but it does not help you understand how the model does math. Even if you know what each neuron does within math, this is remarkably little of the puzzle.

Even if we now have the opportunity to open the box and look inside and know the meaning of individual neurons, that does not tell you how the box fits together, and you are running out of time before the AI is out of the box.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2110804218005332178">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a> (replying to Roon’s claim tup top hat this is a big freaking deal): If you think there is any possible level of interpretability that makes “align ASI” be “normal science” rather than “maaaybe possible” then you do not understand what ASI is or don’t believe in it.

As you know, I now and then post that some research development is unexpectedly good news. But wow, you sure are not selling me on the research or your sanity by proclaiming that it’s gonna make alignment be normal.

Okay, so now I’m looking at there and it’s “polysemanticity and superposition convinced me interpretability was hard, but with big networks there’s single neurons after all” and do. do you have any concept of what utterly. fucking. primitive. playgrounds you are still playing around in. “We thought we might have a hard time finding single neurons that light up for the Eiffel Tower and would have to look at *multiple* neurons, but now maybe we can find single neurons again!”

Do you have any fucking clue of how close this is to the absolute first beginning of understanding how a mind is thinking? Do you think mechanistic interpretability at the level of “do alignment” is about noticing that particular kinds of concepts fired?

To be clear, I did not spot in my skim of the post any grandiose claims about making alignment be normal and my headdesking here is about Roon and not about the researchers. I continue to support interpretability as a field. I would like people to stop overstating where it got.

<a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2111258991762583697">j⧉nus</a>: He’s right, actually, but I think it’s okay that we’re so far from the fantasy of total reductionist understanding. And it’s good that there is progress, even though it’s far, and other kinds of progress are more important.

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2110874411859595426">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: People in 2014: Why aren’t you SUPER EXCITED about interpretability?
Me: Well I yelled at OpenPhil that if they could spend $1B on it they should, but they apparently can’t, which favors my expectation that interpretability just runs much much too slowly to make ASI alignment work.
AI guys: We built a SCREWDRIVER! Now we are going to pry the panel off the computer tower and look inside at all the wheels and gears so we can see how computers work!
Me: I think that you having built the screwdriver so you can *start* looking at things is not as much progress toward the end goal as you seem to believe. It reads as sheer naivete about how much work there is to do, because you don’t concretely know about any of the things you need to figure out. It’s like saying that neuroscience is gonna be solved as soon as you figure out how to look at a neuron with a microscope. This attitude of yours does not inspire confidence.
AI guys: Our SCREWDRIVER BROKE! Neurons do more than one thing! More than one neuron does a thing!
Me: Uh huh.
AI guys in 2026: We FIXED THE SCREWDRIVER! Panel’s coming off the computer tower! Alignment is AS GOOD AS SOLVED!
Crowd: What excellent news! Will you not join us in celebration?

<a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2110853225608106109">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: And it *would* be cool to know how the machine worked. But hearing back a couple of years later that they’ve repaired the screwdriver they were gonna use to maybe get started on looking so they could get started on understanding, does not make me believe the world is now saved.

<a href="https://x.com/ben_r_hoffman/status/2111153335768428828">@ben_r_hoffman</a>: This is in agreement with a strict literal construal of Roon’s tweet. He says your pdoom will be lowered, not low, and that this is the beginning and not the end of making alignment a normal science, and you agree that fixing the screwdriver is an improvement.</blockquote>
The crowd is correct that this is excellent news. It is vastly better to have a screwdriver, even at a late hour, than to not have one. That does not mean anything is ‘as good as solved’ or on track to getting there, or that solving the puzzle would then solve the alignment problem.
<blockquote>Eliezer Yudkowsky:

Me: My pessimism was not based on predicting they’d never get this far. It is based on how much work I believe remains from here.
Idiots: Behold, Yudkowsky NEVER FAVORABLY UPDATES!
Me: Demonstrably false. Here’s me favorably updating in 2025 when the news of a central good-evil axis came out. Are you going to change any of your opinions now?
Idiots: But Yudkowsky doesn’t hugely update when *I* hugely update! No doubt this is… because… Yudkowsky is stupid! Yes, that is undoubtedly the explanation.
Me: It’s just not surprising that they got this far. Like so many other things you are running around waving your arms frantically about having just seen, I already lived through this moment a thousand times in prescience.
Idiots: VERY STUPID!</blockquote>
On the one hand, yes this is strawmanning. On the other hand, why are half the men around me made of straw? So, sigh.

By default, we get an explanation of what an AI just did, or what concepts it was thinking about and how they relate, but without being able to predict outputs.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2110837390780907689">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Me: Mechanistic interpretability sounds like a good idea, yes. If we could learn something about how an AI was thinking, in enough detail to predict in advance what it would think even as the thought departed from human, that would be a bare beginning on the sort of understanding you’d needed to get started on AI alignment that wasn’t just about hammering on an AI from the outside with SFT and RL.
AI guys: We found a neuron that lights up when the AI thinks of the Eiffel Tower!
Me: Cool. That does sound like it’d be a prerequisite to getting started on the important part eventually, someday.
AI guys: Oh NO! There are MULTIPLE neurons that light up when the AI thinks about the Eiffel Tower! And some of those neurons also participate in OTHER concepts! We’re calling this unexpected development ‘polysemanticity’ and ‘superposition’.
Me: That sure sounds like a thing that would happen, yes. I cannot say that I am impressed by you acting surprised about it. Let me know when you’ve gotten far enough in mechanistic interpretability to get started on the important part.
AI guys: We have found that with BIG AIs you can often point to a SINGLE neuron that lights up when the AI thinks of the Eiffel Tower! Understanding AI thought processes is gonna be EASY PEASY!
Me: Cool. Let me know when you’ve advanced to the point of getting started.</blockquote>
Eliezer would be hopeful about interpretability if it allowed us to start making predictions. Until then, he thinks it is not worth so much. I see his point.


<h4>That Was Not the Hard Problem</h4>
This is another way of making Eliezer’s central point: Enumerating the neurons, even if you can do it, is a small fraction of the overall problem. It was not the main barrier. Taking down or lowering difficulty of any barrier is great, but only goes so far. Okay, so there is a lot less intense superposition, now what?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/JakeMendel99/status/2110853286911734014/history">Jake Mendel</a>: After reading for 15 mins: I think Nick is operating in a world where interpretability was just about enumerating the neurons and superposition was an obstacle to that plan, so now he’s happy because maybe superposition isn’t real. But:

1) there are v few empirical results. Unfortunately interp has a long history of significantly overclaiming how interpretable their findings are (SAEs, early circuits having poor causal scrubbing scores etc) so I’m not updating much at all on the evidence he’s already shown. it’s really got a long history of people getting overexcited. So looking forward to seeing the paper with all the empirical evidence!

2) I think it’s not that big news for me even if true because superposition in something like the average case wasn’t my main crux to thinking interpretability is tractable. some cruxes this doesn’t really deal with at all:
– I think the correct ‘circuit’ is usually just an overwhelmingly large bag of random-seeming heuristics (a la Redwood Research’s interpretation of how an LLM predicts is vs was) and having it isn’t very informative/doesn’t help with much/doesn’t lead to good predictions.
– More generally, It seems possible to enumerate all the ‘low level’ features in a neural network, and still have a hard time answering ‘high level’ questions like: “Is the model misaligned? / What would it do in this new situation? / If it is misaligned what should we do about it?” there’s an abstraction/emergence gap to cross from the features to the questions of interest, and crossing that gap is likely much harder than enumerating features.

That said, if his claims hold up, I think it will be quite a big deal, and a useful precursor to tackling the thornier problems! There may also be some useful things to do without tackling the thorny problems: eg we’ll be able to make use of masses of automated agents to scour the features and circuits to try and make sense of the mess, and maybe that’s good for something. although I suspect that even the most optimistic outcome here will not make alignment of superhuman AIs much easier (as some on twitter have claimed) because the neuron interpretability will not be adversarially robust, among other issues</blockquote>

<h4>More Skepticism</h4>
My overall view is remarkably similar to Neel Nanda’s here, although I am modestly more optimistic about this mattering. Looking at individual neurons for primary factors will be useful for some purposes, but you are still missing a lot of what is happening, there will be errors, and this is not altering the big picture by so much.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/NeelNanda5/status/2110875974296654062">Neel Nanda</a>: This is interesting work, but I don’t think it’s a significant update to the big picture of interpretability.

I’m on board with the broad idea of doing exploratory interpretability with neurons, e.g. neuron-based attribution graphs, for pragmatic problems like why did a model do a sketchy thing? I think this work provides some reasonable evidence, in addition to good earlier work from
Transluce, that this might actually work for practical purposes, and has some advantages over the standard alternatives like SAEs/transcoders.

I do not agree with some people’s grander inferences like that this is substantial progress towards ambitious reverse engineering, solving interpretability etc.

I expect that a neuron-based attribution graph will still have significant error and be missing a lot just like transcoders, once you prune them down to the most important components, even if they solve error terms. Which is fine for pragmatic purposes, and may suffice for getting the gist of what’s going on, but I remain pessimistic about really fully understanding things and being confident there’s nothing we’re missing.

… I still think superposition is real and this work doesn’t really affect my cruxes. Most importantly, it seems highly likely to be true that the model can represent more concepts in the residual stream than it has dimensions, ie representational superposition, which I think the authors agree with me on.

For neuron/computational superposition, it seems to me like language models have far more facts and memorized things in them than they have neurons.

And that superposition is an efficient way to pack in more facts than you have neurons by representing them as linear combinations of neurons, especially for arbitrary unstructured values. Facts/memorized content is largely an unstructured database of key-value pairs IMO and I don’t see how this is compatible with a monosemantic view of neurons.</blockquote>
You should be able to store a lot more facts via key-value pairs than you have neurons because neurons each have a lot of associated values? Method seems obvious, although this does not invalidate Neel’s central point. I’m torn on Neel’s later (unquoted here) claim that most memorization is de facto ‘random data.’
<blockquote>I expect that weak neuron activations contain lots of semantic superposition-y info, which, if ablated, would meaningfully damage network performance. As I understand the work, it does not properly address what’s going on with weak neuron activations in LLMs (I am skeptical of how much vision results will transfer, eg I expect small image models memorise far less).

This picture is compatible with the highest activations of a neuron being mono semantic (though IMO the post defines monosemantic too loosely, I think I’d call a fair few of those polysemantic). It’s also plausible that the most important concepts are likely to correspond to a neuron (or a handful), which would be great, and may suffice for practical purposes!

Similarly, my impression is that SAEs generally don’t find things that are a
single neuron, or even very sparse in the neuron basis, and are often a better basis for certain tasks than the neuron basis, though the evidence base is kinda weak here since we largely focus on residual stream or layer output SAEs, I’d love to see work on this in Goodfire’s forthcoming paper! I also think residual stream SAEs are extremely useful, as they give a good bottleneck of the model’s information at this token and layer, and I don’t see a good way to replicate them in the neuron framework.

And kudos to Nick for updating a bunch of his historical vision work! Revisiting old results with modern tools and more rigour seems great.</blockquote>
<a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/2111073786124705886">Nick responded directly</a>, including claiming weak activations are ‘surprisingly ablateable,’ and they went back and forth.

You also can and should start out skeptical on priors. Most hyped results are hyped.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/SamuelAlbanie/status/2111104069221720130/history">Samuel Albanie</a> (Google DeepMind): on base rates, I’m a little skeptical of big positive updates on mech interp. On the other hand, orders of magnitude more ai labour will be coming online in the near future, so the Hamming adage applies:

“In almost all fields, a factor of ten means fundamentally new effects. If you increase magnification by a factor of 10 in Biology, you will see new things.”

so it doesn’t seem implausible that scaling the simple approach will work. will be watching closely.</blockquote>
The bull case is a combination of ‘we could not do this at scale before and now we can’ with ‘it turns out the neurons mostly have singular explanations, so we can do it at all,’ bitter lesson, go. Seems plausible, so long as you do not get overexcited.


<h4>That’s All There Is, There Ain’t No More</h4>
If minds are largely neurons representing specific central concepts, that then have associations with other concepts in these atomic fashions, one might ask ‘<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2111146469814329824">is that all</a>?’
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/TheZvi/status/2111138195429863451">Zvi Mowshowitz</a>: Still reading through this and processing. I’m less struck by the ‘now we can do interpretability easier’ part – you still have to understand what you see – and more by the ‘so you are saying this is all there is to intelligence?’ part. That’s it?

<a href="https://x.com/ElijahRavitz/status/2111198742183633120">Elijah Ravitz-Campbell</a>: I feel like this very much matches how I imagined AI might work before I knew anything about it in practice. A sort of hand wavy “you know concepts have nodes and relationships between those nodes in code semantic knowledge”.

<a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2111146469814329824">roon</a>: Yeah I’m surprised so few are grappling with the philosophical implications of all this

<a href="https://x.com/tessera_antra/status/2111145315071898039">antra</a>: I don’t think that’s it, and I think this is kind of why SD is a lot more interpretable than transformers. This applies to some degree to parts of perceptive, semantic and motoric representations of language models, but not as much to compositional, integrative and temporal ones.

I am also quite sus of weak activation dismissal on strongly recurrent networks like transformers. We saw weak activations matter strongly specifically in the temporal/recureent domain, which is ignored by this paper.</blockquote>
Does this mean that perhaps <a href="https://x.com/BaigYasa/status/2110843522136027504">a dumber mind can understand a smarter one</a>, if all the components are understandable, the same way we can understand complex biological systems, or the components of complex systems or organizations? Certainly you can ‘understand’ something that has advantages over you. If the AI is faster than you, or can memorize more things, it is easy to see how you can understand and even predict it. If intelligence were sufficiently a pure ‘more simple things combined’ in some sense, then you might have a shot when moving at slow speed? That likely presumes that the ‘real action’ is in the neurons, or other places we can parse. We should not assume that.

Can we even simulate a neuron?
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/nabla_theta/status/2110862078844272677">Leo Gao</a> (OpenAI): this is cool work, but i think people are overupdating on how optimistic to be about interp.

in particular, we need to more rigorously evaluate explanations, rather than just going off vibes. imo if you can’t simulate a neuron you haven’t really explained it. my guess is simulating the neurons is way harder than finding plausible explanations. I have a post on this in the works.

(i know the blog talks argues simulations are bad, but i think all of the downsides described are avoided by looking at KL instead of neuron correlation.)

<a href="https://x.com/nickcammarata/status/2110862582877942085">Nick</a>: would love to read it when it’s ready. I haven’t thought enough about simulation scores to be super confident, and could somewhat easily be convinced back</blockquote>
My understanding is that previously we only found ~1.7% of neurons interpretable back in 2023 (Bills et al) but that the test for this required predicting activations. Nick is not yet trying to predict activations.


<h4>The Default Outcome of Mech Interp is Goodhart’s Law</h4>
What happens if we apply optimization pressure to which neurons light up?

Depending on strong or direct the optimization pressure gets, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-most-forbidden-technique">that could be The Most Forbidden Techniqu</a>e.
<blockquote>An AI produces a final output [X] via some method [M]. You can analyze [M] using technique [T], to learn what the AI is up to. You could train on that. Never do that.

You train on [X]. Only [X]. Never [M], never [T].

Why? Because [T] is how you figure out when the model is misbehaving.

If you train on [T], you are training the AI to obfuscate its thinking, and defeat [T]. You will rapidly lose your ability to know what is going on, in exactly the ways you most need to know what is going on.

Those bits of optimization pressure from [T] are precious. Use them wisely.</blockquote>
We must continue to never, ever train on the interpretability, and to watch out for other indirect ways we are doing so. There will be crazy amounts of pressure to pull out forms of The <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-most-forbidden-technique">Most Forbidden Technique</a>.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/SimonLermenAI/status/2111158966856060976">Simon Lermen</a>: Fundamentally, trying to automate alignment research points an objective at “convince the human” for the AI to optimize for. If this plan was to be taken even a little serious you’d expect the AI labs to have very seriously thought about solid metrics and benchmarks that aren’t just going to get goodhearted immediately. Instead we will likely see a last-minute vibe-coded “alignment solution” born from the lethal arrogance of the AI lunatics.</blockquote>
I don’t think this is ‘lethal arrogance of the AI lunatics.’ I think it is that the AI creators are doing the best they can given that they are under extreme time pressure, so everything is always last-minute vibe coded. Once you vibe code it then that becomes the last minute.

My baseline scenario is that the labs will not do the maximally stupid direct optimization thing, but that some others will, and that the labs will end up doing a bunch of indirect optimizations that exert pressure. How much damage results from that pressure will remain unclear.

One way I would expect AIs to adapt to this pressure is to learn how to think about [X] without triggering the direct neurons or detectors for [X]. Do not rely on your detectors in ways that presume future AIs won’t learn to get around them.

We already see places the AI could hide such thinking. There are 2%-5% of unexplained polls, and weak activations look like they could be cheaply ablated and thus are available for other things, plus we don’t understand the cache or attention.


<h4>Interpretability is Dual Use</h4>
One must also remember that essentially everything in alignment is dual use.
<blockquote><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2110869914236158205">Roon</a>: pretraining science is kind of like how people built quite good steam engines before they understood thermodynamics. “Science owes more to the steam engine than the steam engine owes to science.”-(L.J. Henderson). if nick is right, which i am pretty sure he is, progress points in the direction of more interpretability, and pretraining was “slouching towards monosemanticity”. my guess would be that major breakthroughs in interp also advance capabilities​

<a href="https://x.com/NathanpmYoung/status/2110883784640049344">Nathan</a>: If ‘positive’ updates like this are, as roon says, likely to increase capabilities, then funding safety work has an unclear effect on safety.</blockquote>
Interpretability is even more dual use than usual. I still strongly support working on it, but very obviously if you can figure out what the neurons do, or better yet how the whole operation works and thinks, you can use that to increase capabilities. A lot of my first thoughts were how to use this information to improve training, which could then be spent on any combination of seeking alignment or seeking other things.


<h4>Now The Work Can Begin</h4>
What does this all add up to?

Partly that will boil down to how much of this holds up. We have not yet seen the technical paper.

I think this news represents a substantial update in the difficulty of neuron interpretability, and in the ways in which LLMs organize their information and thoughts. This should make it much easier to attack interpretability through a neuron analysis lens than previously thought, and open up a lot of good research and understanding.

I do not think this mean interpretability, let alone alignment, is on its way to being solved. We should be pessimistic about how far such claimed breakthroughs go, the same way SAEs are useful but not as useful as some hoped. Even if they are fully real and work out about maximally well, this is the beginning of the work, not the end, and many things are missing.

We still need to know what is up with weak activations, with the cache and with attention. We still will have little idea how the AIs are thinking, or why they are able to do the breakthroughs and impressive feats. We will not have any guarantee that, if in a hostile situation, our techniques would hold, especially against future AIs, even before we apply optimization pressure over time.

We also need to worry the result largely will not hold up. The Colorado-Tornado result suggests that the whole thing might be a lot less clean than it appears.

I updated a substantial negative amount from the eagerness to jump on the hype train. It pattern matched quite a lot with ‘we will once again pretend we will have everything handled and therefore only invest in these prosaic plans right until we run into another wall and realize this is insufficient.’ We need to fight hard against that.

This is still excellent news. The world is more hopeful now. Let’s get to work.<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/w2tBHwNERDoSawznx/i-can-see-everything#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Sorry, you are still dead](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FjSCpu2B36SAcZL8v/sorry-you-are-still-dead)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>Epistemic status: A brief exploration based on court documents and contemporary reporting. My interpretation is tentative.</em></p>
<p><em>AI use: context research, grammar.</em></p>
<p>I don't remember how I stumbled upon the topic of legally dead people who are still alive. Basically, they are alive, but according to the government, they are dead. I was curious to examine documented examples and discovered Donald Miller's case.</p>
<p>In 1986, Donald Miller disappears from Arcadia, Ohio, leaving two daughters from a previous marriage without his child support. Before disappearing, he lost his job and was struggling with alcoholism. In 1994, his ex-wife obtains a court declaration of his death so their daughters can receive Social Security survivor benefits.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>In the 2000s—news reports say 2005; his affidavit suggests 2008—Donald returns to Ohio, alive. In 2013, he asks the court to undo the declaration and restore his Social Security number. Without it, he says he cannot work or obtain a driver's license.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span> His ex-wife objects, fearing she would have to repay the benefits.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>However, the judge interprets Ohio Revised Code §2121.08(A) as allowing reversal only:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>within a three-year period from the date of the decree</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The clock starts when the declaration is issued, not when the deceased discovers they have been declared deceased. So the court denies Donald's request.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>Despite this, in 2014 Social Security demands that his daughters repay the benefits. By December, it drops those demands.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>Miller's lawyer argues that the deadline concerns estate administration and property. Miller isn't trying to recover property; he just wants to be recognized as legally alive. However, this isn't enough for the judge.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-6">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-6">[6]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>It feels like the crux is how this deadline interacts with §2121.01, which allows a person to be presumed dead after five years of absence without being heard from.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-7">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-7">[7]</a></sup>
    </span> The world was also much less connected in 1994. I imagine verifying whether someone was alive was harder then.</p>
<p>It is also interesting how the court is bound by the law. Here it reaches absurd levels: Miller is alive in front of the judge, yet the judge says the law requires him to uphold the declaration. I guess if you decouple legal death from actual death and treat it as a special state in the system, it feels OK. Still, it feels bizarre. Most people would assume a living person couldn't be declared legally dead.</p>
<p>I can also understand where the five-year rule comes from: some people die without leaving a recoverable body. Their families still need a way to settle their affairs.</p>
<p>U.S. military law explicitly handles people declared dead returning alive, even providing back pay in eligible cases.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-8">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-8">[8]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p>Overall, this is an interesting example of trying to squeeze the world's complexity into a formal, well-bounded and understandable system, and how the system breaks at its corner cases.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-1"><p><a href="https://www.upi.com/Odd_News/2013/10/09/Man-unable-to-get-his-death-ruling-overturned/15901381332824/">UPI, “Man unable to get his death ruling overturned” (October 9, 2013)</a>. Reports his disappearance, job loss, alcoholism, the purpose of the 1994 declaration, and his reported return in 2005. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-2"><p><a href="https://probate.co.hancock.oh.us/cgi-bin/imageret.cgi?check=PCPR33251050145FE1991401&amp;imgext=pdf">Miller's complaint and sworn affidavit, filed July 30, 2013</a>, Exhibit A, paragraphs 2–4. The affidavit says he learned of the declaration “five (5) years ago upon his return to Ohio,” suggesting 2008 rather than the 2005 reported by UPI. It also describes his canceled Social Security number and inability to obtain employment or an Ohio driver's license. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-3"><p><a href="https://www.pressherald.com/2013/10/11/ohio-judge-tells-man-hes-still-legally-dead/">Associated Press, “Ohio judge tells man he's still legally dead” (October 11, 2013)</a>. Reports his former wife's opposition and concern that she could not afford to repay the benefits. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-4"><p><a href="https://codes.ohio.gov/ohio-revised-code/section-2121.08">Ohio Revised Code §2121.08(A)</a>. The judge expressly applied the three-year deadline in the <a href="https://probate.co.hancock.oh.us/cgi-bin/imageret.cgi?check=PCPR33242050723E1991D401&amp;imgext=pdf">judgment filed October 17, 2013</a>, following the October 7 hearing. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-5"><p><a href="https://www.loweringthebar.net/2014/08/feds-say-legally-dead-is-slightly-alive.html">Kevin Underhill, “Feds Say Legally Dead Is Slightly Alive” (August 18, 2014, with a December 2014 update)</a>. Summarizes local reporting that Social Security demanded $47,256 from the daughters and subsequently dropped the demands. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-6"><p><a href="https://probate.co.hancock.oh.us/cgi-bin/imageret.cgi?check=PCPR33241050B596199B401&amp;imgext=pdf">Miller's memorandum opposing dismissal, filed October 3, 2013</a>, paragraphs 2–5. His lawyer argues that the statute concerns estate and property matters and should not bar recognition that Miller is alive. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-7"><p><a href="https://codes.ohio.gov/ohio-revised-code/section-2121.01">Ohio Revised Code §2121.01(A)(1)</a>. Subsection (A)(2) also permits a presumption after a shorter absence when the person was exposed to a specific peril of death. Five years is therefore not a universal waiting period for every disappearance. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-8"><p><a href="https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/10/1511">10 U.S.C. §1511(a)</a>. Covers people officially missing or declared dead under the specified federal provisions who return alive to U.S. control. It provides pay for the period of absence, subject to the applicable rules, and excludes people subsequently determined to have been absent without leave or deserters. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-caFiXEkwJApRQNm7C-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FjSCpu2B36SAcZL8v/sorry-you-are-still-dead#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Chain of Thought For Humans](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FZ6FPNCfqikNgbyef/chain-of-thought-for-humans)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These past few weeks, I’ve been conducting interviews, and I often open with “make sure you articulate your thoughts out loud, so I can follow along with your thinking”. This is primarily because I’m not a mind reader, but it does also help ensure candidates avoid freezing up. It’s a very “interviewy” skill though - artificial and unlike real development.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The other thing I’ve been doing is playing </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Witness_(2016_video_game)"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Witness</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a 2016 puzzle game centred around solving mazes with increasingly complicated hidden rules. The game has a unexciting presentation, no music or interesting dialog, and no story. At first I found the game fairly dull, you spend a lot of it just wrestling with possibilities and combinatorics. It can get very frustrating when you are not making progress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But, inspired by my interviews, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’ve started narrating my own thought process out loud to myself</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I look like a bit of a loon, but I tell myself I’m just a streamer with an audience of one. And to my surprise, it’s working really well. Not only does it make the game more lively and energetic, but also I find </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I’m more effective in solving puzzles</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I was initially baffled by this. Common sense says that I can’t articulate my thoughts at remotely the same speed or accuracy as thinking them. Particularly for a puzzle game where the majority of reasoning is geometric, difficult to describe in speech. We know LLMs benefit from this sort of reasoning, but that is due to an architectural limitation - they literally cannot iterate on previous thoughts; they must output tokens to get any true recursion.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But then I remembered </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rubber_duck_debugging"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rubber duck debugging</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It’s often useful in software engineering to explain your problem to someone else. But who you explain to isn’t really an important part of the process - it could be a rubber duck. The value is in forcing you to evaluate all your unstated assumptions, linearly sort your thoughts by priority and dependency, and find good abstractions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are also useful skills for real-time problem solving. I think there are some further benefits for this particular use case:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It forces you to establish named formal rules and concepts, instead of relying purely on building intuition. In other words, engaging </span><a href="https://thedecisionlab.com/reference-guide/philosophy/system-1-and-system-2-thinking"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">System II</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which the puzzles are explicitly designed to require.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It forces you to acknowledge when you are making an arbitrary choice. Useful for backtracking or exhaustively searching a possibility space.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it lets you use </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echoic_memory"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">echoic memory</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to slightly augment your working memory.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I may well have accidentally triggered the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawthorne_effect"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hawthorne effect</span></a></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It turns out there’s good evidence that this technique has a moderate benefit, for example this </span><a href="https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s10648-018-9434-x"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">meta-study</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (n=6000 participants). I’m definitely going to try it in future in more circumstances.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FZ6FPNCfqikNgbyef/chain-of-thought-for-humans#comments">Discuss</a>

## Engineering

### [When Software Meets Hardware](https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>When software meets hardware, engineering principles remain largely the
    same, but the sensible defaults often change. <b class="author">Kuldeep Singh</b>
    describes our experience modernizing a large IoT fleet of battery-powered
    sensors. Our familiar principles remained important; what changed was how we
    applied them. These practices emerged through experiments, failures, and
    operational experience&#x2014;each challenge helping us replace a reasonable
    assumption with a more sensible default. </p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/software-meets-hardware.html">more…</a></p>

### [Build your slide template](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Building a slide template starts with three foundational choices &#x2014;
      colours, fonts and layouts. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> covers
      the basics of color theory and how to build a reusable palette, the
      essentials of typography, including serif, sans-serif, handwritten and
      monospace fonts, plus the finer points of kerning and leading, and why a
      near-blank layout might serve you better than the prescribed layouts that
      ship with your slideware.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/build-your-slide-template.html">more…</a></p>

### [The Craft of Practitioner Voice](https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A couple of months ago <b class="author">Jim Highsmith</b> explained how
      practitioners writing for each other forms a distinct style of writing. He
      now adds a section to the article with more detail on the craft of how we
      write in this style. How the writer shows up in the prose, its four
      principles, the role of story, how to sensibly use AI, three levels of
      editing, and clarity of the audience.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/practitioner-voice.html#TheCraftOfPractitionerVoice">more…</a></p>

### [Are Harnesses Worth the Cost?](https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/donkey-card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p><b class="author">Naresh Jain</b> compares coding agents building the same
      API service with and without a behavioral harness. A frontier model, GPT-6
      Sol, passed every external evaluation either way. But a cost-optimized
      model, GPT-6 Luna, succeeded in only 3 of 10 unharnessed runs and all 10
      with the harness. While adding the harness increased token usage and
      runtime for Luna, the harnessed Luna runs achieved the same 10-for-10
      reliability as GPT-6 Sol while costing 33% less per run ($0.273 vs
      $0.406). This experiment suggests a behavioral harness might be a
      practical way to get dependable results from smaller, cost-optimized
      models.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/are-harnesses-worth-the-cost.html">more…</a></p>

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

---

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>
